A great discovery solves a great problem, but in the solution
tion of every problem, there is a certain discovery. The problem that
poses can be modest; p"to, if it tests the curiosity that induces
to put inventive faculties into play, if it is resolved by one's own means.
God, you can experience the charm of discovery and the joy of
triumph. Experiences of this type, at a suitable age, can determine
mine a penchant for intellectual work and leave an impeccable imprint on it.
recede in mind and character.
Therefore, a mathematics teacher has a great opportunity' Yes
He dedicates his time to exercising students in routine operations, mataúr
interest in them will prevent their intellectual development and they will end up wasting
taking your chance. But if, on the contrary, it tests curiosity,
ity of their students by posing problems appropriate to their knowledge,
and helps them solve them through stimulating questions, you will be able
awaken in them a taste for independent thinking and provide them
certain resources for this.
A student whose studies include some degree of mathematics also
also a particular opportunity. This opportunity is lost, of course.
is, if you see mathematics as a subject that you have to present
a final exam and which will not be dealt with again once this exam has passed.
The opportunity may be lost even if the student has a talent
natural for mathematics, since he, like anyone else, must discover
share your abilities and your hobbies; can't tell if he likes cake
of raspberries if you have never tried it. You may discover, however, that
A math problem can be as much or more fun than a math problem.
cigratna., or that vigorous intellectual work can be such an exercise
pleasant like a quick game of tennis. Having tasted the pleasure of
mathematics, and will not easily forget them, presenting then a

The author remembers the time when he was a student, a student a
both ambitious, with the desire to penetrate a little into mathematics and
physics. I attended conferences, read books, trying to assimilate the solutions
and the facts presented, but there was always an interrogante that
disturbed him incessantly: "yes, the solution given to the problem seems to be
correct, but how is it possible to discover such a solution? yes, this one experienced
apparently it is correct, it seems to be a fact; but how can they
such facts be discovered? and how can I by myself invent or
discover such things?" Today the author teaches mathematics in a
university. Do you think or wish that some of your most gifted students
Ask yourself similar questions and try to satisfy your curiosity. Trying
to understand not only the solution to this or that problem, but also
also the reasons and the procedure of the solution, and trying to
to understand these reasons and procedures, has been carried out
I am finally going to write this book. You want it to be useful
to those teachers who want to develop the skills of their students
to solve problems, and for those students eager to develop
their own abilities.
Although this book pays special attention to the requirements
of mathematics students and teachers, should awaken the
interest of all those interested in the ways and means of invention.
tion and discovery. Such interest may be greater than that
one can suspect without prior reflection. The space dedicated to
newspapers and magazines to crosswords and other riddles seems to demonstrate
that the public spends a certain amount of time solving problems without any
practical interest. Behind the desire to solve this or that problem that
does not provide any material advantage, there must be a deep curiosity, a
desire to understand the ways and means, the motives and procedures of
the solution.

facet of mathematics. Indeed, mathematics has two faces:
on the one hand they are the rigorous science of Euclid, but they are also something
more. Mathematics presented in the Euclidean way looks like
a systematic, deductive science; but mathematics on the way

MAIN DIVISIONS, MAIN QUESTIONS
6. four phases. In trying to find the solution we can repeatedly change our point of view, our way of considering the problem.
We have to change positions over and over again. Our conception of the problem will probably be incomplete when we start working; our vision will be different when we are further along and will change again when we are ready to achieve the solution.
In order to conveniently group the questions and suggestions on our list, we will distinguish four phases of the work. first, to understand the problem, that is, to see clearly what I asked for. Secondly, we have to grasp the relationships that exist between the different elements, see what links the unknown with other data in order to find the idea of a solution and be able to draw up a plan. Third, implement pran. Fourth, once the solution is found, review it and repeat it. Each of these important functions can happen to a student.
:_ j: 1",::"r.1¡o,t chance an exceptionally clever idea and skipping all the preparatory work, go directly to the solution. Such strokes of luck are naturally desirable, but an undesired, unfortunate result can be reached if the group takes care of four phases without having a good idea. to oppose this type of "aid". In effect:

or "Brilliant idea", as well as "good idea", are common expressions

knowledge familiar to everyone, but difficult to describe. For this reason we Pa-
It is interesting to quote a suggestive description of it, given inci-
,dentally by an authority as ancient as Aristotle.
It will be generally agreed that conceiving a brilliant idea is an "act
of sagacity." Aristotle defines "sagacity" as follows: "Sagacity is
discover by guessing an essential relationship in an unappreciated period of time
. ciable. So, for example, if you see someone talking in a certain way
to a rich man, you can instantly guess that the person
is trying to borrow money. Or, noting that the part
r bright moon is always facing the sun, you can d9 soon know
the why; that is, because the moon is illuminated by the light of the sun." *
The first example, without being bad, is somewhat trivial. Not required
much sagacity to suppose something of this kind about the rich and the
money. The idea is not very bright. The second example, on the contrary,
It is remarkable if we make a small effort of imagination to place it
on his own stage.
It must be kept in mind that in Aristotle's time it was necessary to observe
change the sun and the stars if you wanted to know the time, since clocks
They did not exist, and you had to observe the phases of the moon if you wanted to travel
at night, since there were no public lighting. It was, therefore, in
at that time very familiar with the sky that an inhabitant of the
city of today, and his natural intelligence was not falsified by
the mediocre assimilation of journalistic articles spreading astronomical theories
nomics. They saw the moon as a flat disk, similar to the disk of the sun, but
much less bright. They must have been amazed at the incessant changes.
bios of shapes and positions of the moon. Occasionally they watched her
in the light of day, towards dawn or towards dusk, and they discovered "that the
bright side of the moon is always towards the sun", which constitutes for
them a respectable achievement. They now conceive that the variant aspects of the
moon are similar to those of a ball that was illuminated from one side
such that half remained illuminated and the other half dark. From there the
conception of the sun and the moon not as flat discs, but as bodies
round, one giving and the other receiving the light. It is then understood

6. Multiple definitions. The sphere is usually defined as the place
geometric arrangement of points located at a given distance from a point
given. (Points are now considered in space, not restricted to
a plan ) It can also be defined as the surface generated by a
circle that rotates around one of its diameters. There are also
other definitions of the sphere, and many others would be possible.
When the problem to be solved concerns some derived notion,
as the "sphere" or the "parabola" and we want to refer to its definition,
There is a choice between several definitions. A lot can depend then
of the choice of the definition appropriate to the case.
Determining the surface of the sphere was an important problem and
difficult at the time Archimedes solved it. Archimedes had to
choose between the definitions we have just given. He preferred to conceive the
fqra as the surface generated by a circle rotating around a
fixed diameter. He inscribed in the circle a regular polygon, of a number
pair of sides, two of whose opposite vertices were joined by the fixed diameter
of the circle. The regular polygon approaches circle I, rotating with é1,
generates a convex surface composed of a part of two cones
whose vertices are at the extreme points of the fixed diameter, and on the other hand
part, between the cones, of several truncated cones. This composite surface
approximately equals that of the sphere and Archimedes used it to

Auxiliary elements. Our conception of the problem is much richer at the end of our work than at the beginning (nnocnrso and LocRo,1.) In effect, as our work pr,.i", we add new elements to those we considered at the starting point. An element that we introduce in the hope that it will help us find the solution is called an attxiliai element.
1. There are various types of auxiliary elements. By solving a geometric problem, we can introduce new lines in the figure; They are auxiliary lines. Solving an algebra problem, we can introduce an auxiliary unknown (nnonrrua auxrrnn, -r). An auxiliary unknown is the one whose proof we try to deduce in order to facilitate the solution of the initial problem.

2. Generalization can be useful in solving certain problems.
more. Consider the following geometry of space: "IJna straight and a
regular octahedron are in a given relative position. Determine a plane
that passes through the straight line and cuts the volume into two equal parts." This pro-
problem may seem difficult, but in fact, a little familiarity with
the shape of the regular ctahedron is enough to bring to mind the
next, more general problem: "A straight line and a solid with center of si-
mería are in a given relative position. Determine a plane that passes
along the straight line and that cuts the solid into two equal parts." The required plan
It naturally passes through the center of symmetry of the solid; is determined
by the point and the given line. Since the octahedron also has a center
of symmetry the original problem is also solved.
The reader should not fail to observe that the second, more general problem
neral than the first is, however, much easier. In fact, the me-
main data that we have used to solve the first problem

4. The preceding observations, however, are subject to certain

A well-posed and reasonable "problem to be solved" must have
all the necessary data without any being superfluous; the condition must
be sufficient without being either contradictory or redundant. When solving a Pfo-
problem of this type, we must naturally use all the data and
complete condition.
The object of a "problem to be proven" is a mathematical theorem.
If the problem is fine

_ Why the demonstrations? A classic anecdote is told about Newton. As a young student, he began the study of geometry, as was customary in his time, by reading Euclid's Elements. He read the theorems, confirmed their accuracy and omitted the demonstrations, wondering why they took so much trouble to demonstrate such obvious truths. Years later, however, he changed his mind and became an admirer of Euclid.
Authentic or not, this anecdote leads us to the next question: why learn or teach proofs? What vate-more, nída demosrrar, Frc. 18Why the illustrations? t49
demonstrated everything or demonstrated in part? And if only a few demonstrations are given
tions, how do you choose?

6. What is progress towards the solution? It is the advancement of
the mobilization and organization of our knowledge, an evolution
of our conception of the problem, a growing forecast of the stages
that will constitute the final reasoning. Advancement may be slow
perceptible, but at times its speed increases abruptly, in jumps. This
Sudden progress toward the solution is called a BRTLLANTE RDEA, a
good idea, a discovery (in German there is a more technical term: Ein-
fail). What is a brilliant idea? It is a sudden and essential transformation
from our point of view, a sudden reorganization of our way
of conceiving the problem, a forecast of the stages that will lead us to the
solution, forecast inwhich, despite its sudden appearance, we sense
that we can trust.

4. Oppositions, The methods we study have found an opposition
considerable position. Many objections have been raised which, probably,
They are nothing more than different forms of the same fundamental objection. Tra-
We will address here in a "practical" way the objection that is situated at our level.
Finding a proof that is not obvious is an intellectual achievement
considerable, but learning it or even thoroughly understanding it requires
also some mental effort. It is very natural that we want to get some
benefit from this effort and, of course, we want to reserve the resources
of our memory for what is true and correct and not for what is false
and absurd.
But it seems difficult to retain anything true from a
duction to the absurd. Hemethod consists of starting from a false hypothesis and

In the course of developing the proof we must remember each
